\qquad
\ell,\rho: i\to j
\]
be morphisms of \(\mathbf H^{\join}_\Sigma\), and suppose that
\[
\mathcal F,\mathcal G:0\to m \coprod n,
\qquad
\mathcal L,\mathcal R:0\to i \coprod j
\]
are well-typed extended cospans such that
\begin{equation}
\mathcal F
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L,\mathcal R\rangle
}
\mathcal G
\tag{4}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_5}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
Q(\mathcal F)&=I(\corner f),
&
Q(\mathcal G)&=I(\corner g),\\
Q(\mathcal L)&=I(\corner \ell),
&
Q(\mathcal R)&=I(\corner \rho).
\end{aligned}
\tag{5}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_6}
\end{equation}
We claim that
\begin{equation}
f \Rightarrow_{\langle \ell,\rho \rangle} g.
\tag{6}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_7}
\end{equation}

We prove the claim by induction on the number of
\(\boxsort\)-edges enclosing the match anchor.

Suppose first that the anchor is the root. By
\cref{lemma:root_edpoi_factorisation}, there is a well-typed extended
cospan
\[
\mathcal C:i \coprod j\longrightarrow m \coprod n
\]
such that
\[
\mathcal F
=
\mathcal L\fatsemi\mathcal C,
\qquad
\mathcal G
=
\mathcal R\fatsemi\mathcal C.
\]
Applying \(Q\) gives
\[
Q(\mathcal F)
=
Q(\mathcal L)\fatsemi Q(\mathcal C),
\qquad
Q(\mathcal G)
=
Q(\mathcal R)\fatsemi Q(\mathcal C).
\]

By fullness of \(I\), choose a morphism
\[
c:i+j \longrightarrow m+n
\]
of \(\mathbf H^{\join}_\Sigma\) such that
\[
I(c) = Q(\mathcal C).
\]
Using~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_6}, we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
I(\corner f)
&=Q(\mathcal F)\\
&=Q(\mathcal L)\fatsemi Q(\mathcal C)\\
&=I(\corner \ell)\fatsemi I(c)\\
&=I(\corner \ell\fatsemi c).
\end{aligned}
\]
Since \(I\) is faithful,
\[
\corner f = \corner \ell \fatsemi c.
\]
The same calculation gives
\[
\corner g = \corner \rho \fatsemi c.
\]

Applying the inverse bending operation to these equalities gives a
common composition-and-tensor context containing \(\ell\), respectively
\(\rho\). Hence the first clause of
\cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting} gives
\[
f\Rightarrow_{\langle\ell,\rho\rangle}g.
\]

Suppose now that the anchor is not the root. Apply
\cref{lemma:outermost_box_decomposition}. Let \(p\) be the outermost
enclosing \(\boxsort\)-edge, let
\[
u\defeq |s(p)|,
\qquad
v\defeq |t(p)|,
\]
and let
\[
e\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)
\]
be the selected component.

For every
\(d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\), fullness of \(I\)
supplies a morphism
\[
f_d:u\to v
\]
such that
\[
I(f_d) = Q(\mathcal F_d).
\]
Likewise, choose
\[
g_e:u\to v
\]
such that
\[
I(g_e) = Q(\mathcal G_e).
\]
Finally, let
\[
\mathcal C : u \coprod v\longrightarrow m \coprod n
\]
be the context supplied by
\cref{lemma:outermost_box_decomposition}. By fullness of \(I\), choose
\[
c : u + v\longrightarrow m + n
\]
such that
\[
I(c) = Q(\mathcal C).
\]

Part~(1) of \cref{lemma:outermost_box_decomposition} gives an EDPOI
rewrite
\[
\mathcal F_e
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L,\mathcal R\rangle
}
\mathcal G_e
\]
whose match has fewer enclosing \(\boxsort\)-edges. 
Since the action of $\corner{-}$ is given by tensoring and composition, this action is preserved by $\overline{\rightsquigarrow}$ by~\cref{lemma:rewriting_congruence} and therefore we get
\begin{equation}
\corner{\mathcal F_e}
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L,\mathcal R\rangle
}
\corner{\mathcal G_e}.
\tag{7}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_8}
\end{equation}

Moreover,
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q(\corner{\mathcal F_e})
&=\corner{Q(\mathcal F_e)}\\
&=\corner{I(f_e)}\\
&=I(\corner f_e),
\end{aligned}
\]
because \(Q\) and \(I\) preserve the cups, tensor and
composition. Similarly,
\[
Q(\corner{\mathcal G_e})
=
I(\corner g_e).
\]
Together with the two rule equalities in~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_6}, these are precisely the
denotation hypotheses of the inductive claim. Since the rewrite in
~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_8} has fewer enclosing boxes, the induction hypothesis gives
\begin{equation}
f_e\Rightarrow_{\langle \ell,\rho \rangle}g_e.
\tag{8}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_9}
\end{equation}

Put
\[
p_F
\defeq
\bigjoin_{d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)}f_d
\]
and
\[
p_G
\defeq
g_e
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
  d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\\
  d\neq e
}}
f_d.
\]
% By~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_9} and the join clause of
% \cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}, we obtain
% \begin{equation}
% p_F \Rightarrow_{\langle \ell,\rho \rangle} p_G.
% \tag{9}
% \label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_10}
% \end{equation}

Recall that
\[
\mathcal P_{\mathcal F}
=
\bigjoin_{d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)}
\mathcal F_d
\]
and
\[
\mathcal P_{\mathcal G}
=
\mathcal G_e
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
  d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\\
  d\neq e
}}
\mathcal F_d.
\]
Since \(Q\) and \(I\) preserve joins,
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q(\mathcal P_{\mathcal F})
&=
\bigjoin_d Q(\mathcal F_d)\\
&=
\bigjoin_d I(f_d)\\
&=
I(p_F),
\end{aligned}
\]
and, similarly,
\[
Q(\mathcal P_{\mathcal G})=I(p_G).
\]
Compatibility with bending therefore gives
\begin{equation}
Q(\corner{\mathcal P_{\mathcal F}})
=
I(\corner p_F),
\qquad
Q(\corner{\mathcal P_{\mathcal G}})
=
I(\corner p_G).
\tag{9}
\label{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_11}
\end{equation}

By \cref{lemma:outermost_box_decomposition},
\[
\mathcal F
=
\corner{\mathcal P_{\mathcal F}}
\fatsemi\mathcal C,
\qquad
\mathcal G
=
\corner{\mathcal P_{\mathcal G}}
\fatsemi\mathcal C.
\]
Applying \(Q\) and using~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_6},~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_11}, and
\(I(c)=Q(\mathcal C)\), we obtain
\[
\begin{aligned}
I(\corner f)
&=Q(\mathcal F)\\
&=
Q(\corner{\mathcal P_{\mathcal F}})
\fatsemi Q(\mathcal C)\\
&=
I(\corner p_F)\fatsemi I(c)\\
&=
I(\corner p_F\fatsemi c).
\end{aligned}
\]
Faithfulness of \(I\) gives
\[
\corner f = \corner p_F \fatsemi c.
\]
The same calculation gives
\[
\corner g =\corner p_G\fatsemi c.
\]

Applying the inverse bending operation to these equalities exhibits
\(f\) and \(g\) in the form of the second decomposition in
\cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}. They have the same outer
composition-and-tensor context and the same unchanged components
\[
\bigjoin_{\substack{
  d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\\
  d\neq e
}}
f_d,
\]
while their distinguished components are \(f_e\) and \(g_e\),
respectively. Since
\[
f_e\Rightarrow_{\langle\ell,\rho\rangle}g_e
\]
by~\eqref{eq:edpoi_rewriting_frob_9}, the second clause of
\cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting} gives
\[
f\Rightarrow_{\langle\ell,\rho\rangle}g.
\]
This completes the induction and proves the claim.

Finally, suppose that
\[
\mathrm{Rep}(\corner a)
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}^{\,\mathfrak R}_{
  \left\langle
    \mathrm{Rep}(\corner l),
    \mathrm{Rep}(\corner r)
  \right\rangle
}
\mathrm{Rep}(\corner b).
\]
Choose witnesses
\[
\mathcal F',\mathcal G',\mathcal L',\mathcal R'
\]
from \cref{def:edpoi_modulo_structural}. Thus
\[
\mathcal F'
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal G'
\]
and
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Rep}(\corner a)&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal F',
&
\mathcal G'&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathrm{Rep}(\corner b),\\
\mathrm{Rep}(\corner l)&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal L',
&
\mathrm{Rep}(\corner r)&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal R'.
\end{aligned}
\]
By \cref{lemma:concrete_interpretation_quotient} and
\cref{lemma:concrete_interpretation_bending},
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q(\mathcal F')
&=Q(\mathrm{Rep}(\corner a))
 =I([\corner a])
 =I(\corner{[a]}),\\
Q(\mathcal G')
&=Q(\mathrm{Rep}(\corner b))
 =I([\corner b])
 =I(\corner{[b]}),\\
Q(\mathcal L')
&=Q(\mathrm{Rep}(\corner l))
 =I([\corner l])
 =I(\corner{[l]}),\\
Q(\mathcal R')
&=Q(\mathrm{Rep}(\corner r))
 =I([\corner r])
 =I(\corner{[r]}).
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying the claim with
\[
f = [a],
\qquad
g = [b],
\qquad
\ell = [l],
\qquad
\rho=[r]
\]
and with the direct EDPOI rewrite
\[
\mathcal F'
\;\overline{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal G'
\]
gives
\[
[a]\Rightarrow_{\langle[l],[r]\rangle}[b],
\]
as required.
\end{proof}

The theorem therefore identifies enriched term rewriting with EDPOI
rewriting modulo the structural rules. 
Symmetric monoidal and Frobenius equations require no explicit structural rewriting, since
they are already satisfied by the concrete cospan interpretation. 
The $\catname{SLat}$-equations are instead absorbed by
$\sim_{\mathfrak R}$, which permits the EDPOI step to be taken between
suitable hierarchical representatives of the source, target and rule.

Importantly, this correspondence does not amount to unfolding every
e-hypergraph and then applying an ordinary join-free rewrite. 
Complete unfolding may erase precisely the join structure needed to expose a
redex. 
For example, if the left-hand side of a rule is
$a\join b$, then bilinearity identifies
\[
f\fatsemi(a\join b)
=
(f\fatsemi a)\join(f\fatsemi b).
\]
In the fully distributed representative, neither summand contains a
single occurrence of the redex $a\join b$. 
Rewriting modulo
$\mathfrak R$ instead permits a structurally equivalent hierarchical
representative in which the relevant join remains grouped inside its
enclosing $\boxsort$-edge. 
The outermost-box decomposition then isolates
the component containing the match while retaining the other
components as the common join context. 
Thus the e-hypergraph
presentation detects enriched redexes at their proper hierarchical
level, rather than only after flattening.

\section{The rewriting correspondence for symmetric monoidal theories}
The developed machinery tranlslates verbatim to the case of $\catname{SLat}$-PROP $\mathbf{S}^{\join}_{\Sigma}$ which lacks the Frobenius structure, by constraining extended cospans, extended pushout complements and EDPOI rewriting similarly to~\cref{def:monogamous_cospans},~\cref{def:boundary_pushout_complement},~\cref{def:convex_dpoi_rewriting}.
We list the corresponding definitions and results next.

\subsection{Monogamous acyclic extended cospans}
\begin{definition}[Monogamous asyclic (MDA-) extended cospans]
An extended cospan from~\cref{def:extended_cospan}:
\[
n \xrightarrow{f_{\mathrm{ext}}} n' \xrightarrow{f_{\mathrm{int}}} \mathcal{F} \xleftarrow{g_{\mathrm{int}}} k' \xleftarrow{g_{\mathrm{ext}}} k
\]
is \emph{monogamous and acyclic} if
\begin{enumerate}
  \item the e-hypergraph \(\mathcal{F}\) is acyclic;
  \item $f_\mathrm{int}$ and $g_{\mathrm{int}}$ are monomorphisms;
  \item in-degree and out-degree of every vertex in \(\mathcal{F}\) is at most one;
  \item vertices with in-degree $0$ are precisely those in the image of $f_{\mathrm{int}}$;
  \item vertices with out-degree $0$ are precisely those in the image of $g_{\mathrm{int}}$.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
Recall that $f_{\mathrm{ext}}$ and $g_{\mathrm{ext}}$ are mono by definition of extended cospans and therefore $f_{\mathrm{ext}}\fatsemi f_{\mathrm{int}}$ and $g_{\mathrm{ext}}\fatsemi g_{\mathrm{int}}$ are also mono.
We will similarly consider only well-typed MDA-extended cospans, i.e., those that additionally satisfy~\cref{def:well_typed_ecospan}.

\begin{definition}
Well-typed MDA-extended cospans form the category that we denote by $\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{Extended boundary pushout complements}
\begin{definition}[Extended boundary pushout complement]
Consider well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\[
i \xrightarrow{f^{i}_{\mathrm{ext}}} i' \xrightarrow{f^{i}_{\mathrm{int}}} \mathcal{L} \xleftarrow{f^{j}_{\mathrm{int}}} j' \xleftarrow{f^{j}_{\mathrm{ext}}} j~,
\]
and
\[
n \xrightarrow{g^{n}_{\mathrm{ext}}} n' \xrightarrow{g^{n}_{\mathrm{int}}} \mathcal{G} \xleftarrow{g^{k}_{\mathrm{int}}} k' \xleftarrow{g^{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}} k~.
\]
together with the diagram as in~\cref{def:extended_complement}, exhibiting $i \coprod j \xrightarrow{[c_1,c_2]} \mathcal{L}^{\bot} \xrightarrow{l^{\bot}} \mathcal{F}$ as an extended pushout complement in $\catname{EHyp}_{\bullet}(\Sigma)$.
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsMTAsWzAsMCwiaSdfe1xcY2lyY30gXFxjb3Byb2Qgaidfe1xcY2lyY30iXSxbMiwwLCJcXG1hdGhjYWx7TH0iXSxbMywwLCJpJyBcXGNvcHJvZCBqJyJdLFs0LDAsImkgXFxjb3Byb2QgaiJdLFswLDIsIlNfbiBcXGNvcHJvZCBTX2siXSxbMiwyLCJcXG1hdGhjYWx7R30iXSxbNCwyLCJcXG1hdGhjYWx7TH1ee1xcYm90fSJdLFszLDMsIm4nIFxcY29wcm9kIGsnIl0sWzQsMywiKG4nIFxcc2V0bWludXMgU19uKSBcXGNvcHJvZCAoaycgXFxzZXRtaW51cyBTX2spIl0sWzQsNSwibiBcXGNvcHJvZCBrIl0sWzAsMSwiW2Zee2l9X3tcXG1hdGhybXtpbnR9fVxccmVzdHJpY3Rpb25fe2knX3tcXGNpcmN9fSwgZl57an1fe1xcbWF0aHJte2ludH19XFxyZXN0cmljdGlvbl97aidfe1xcY2lyY319XSJdLFswLDQsIlxcYWxwaGFfbiBcXGNvcHJvZCBcXGFscGhhX2siLDJdLFsxLDUsIm0iLDJdLFsyLDEsIltmXmlfe1xcbWF0aHJte2ludH19LGZeal97XFxtYXRocm17aW50fX1dIiwyXSxbMywyLCJbZl57aX1fe1xcbWF0aHJte2V4dH19LGZee2p9X3tcXG1hdGhybXtleHR9fV0iLDJdLFszLDYsIltjXzEsY18yXSJdLFs0LDUsIltcXGhhdHtnfV97bn1ee2F9LCBcXGhhdHtnfV97a31ee2F9XSJdLFs1LDMsIiIsMCx7InN0eWxlIjp7Im5hbWUiOiJjb3JuZXIiLCJib2R5Ijp7Im5hbWUiOiJub25lIn0sImhlYWQiOnsibmFtZSI6Im5vbmUifX19XSxbNiw1LCJsXntcXGJvdH0iLDJdLFs3LDUsIltnXntufV97XFxtYXRocm17aW50fX0sZ157a31fe1xcbWF0aHJte2ludH19XSJdLFs4LDYsIltnX25ee1xcYm90fSxnX2tee1xcYm90fV0iLDJdLFs4LDcsIlxcam1hdGgiLDJdLFs5LDcsIltnXntufV97XFxtYXRocm17ZXh0fX0sZ157a31fe1xcbWF0aHJte2V4dH19XSJdLFs5LDgsIltcXG92ZXJsaW5le2d9XntufV97XFxtYXRocm17ZXh0fX0sXFxvdmVybGluZXtnfV57a31fe1xcbWF0aHJte2V4dH19XSIsMl1d
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{i'_{\circ} \coprod j'_{\circ}} && {\mathcal{L}} & {i' \coprod j'} & {i \coprod j} \\
	\\
	{S_n \coprod S_k} && {\mathcal{G}} && {\mathcal{L}^{\bot}} \\
	&&& {n' \coprod k'} & {(n' \setminus S_n) \coprod (k' \setminus S_k)} \\
	\\
	&&&& {n \coprod k}
	\arrow["{[f^{i}_{\mathrm{int}}\restriction_{i'_{\circ}}, f^{j}_{\mathrm{int}}\restriction_{j'_{\circ}}]}", from=1-1, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{\alpha_n \coprod \alpha_k}"', from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["m"', from=1-3, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{[f^i_{\mathrm{int}},f^j_{\mathrm{int}}]}"', from=1-4, to=1-3]
	\arrow["{[f^{i}_{\mathrm{ext}},f^{j}_{\mathrm{ext}}]}"', from=1-5, to=1-4]
	\arrow["{[c_1,c_2]}", from=1-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow["{[\hat{g}_{n}^{a}, \hat{g}_{k}^{a}]}", from=3-1, to=3-3]
	\arrow["\lrcorner"{anchor=center, pos=0.125, rotate=90}, draw=none, from=3-3, to=1-5]
	\arrow["{l^{\bot}}"', from=3-5, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{[g^{n}_{\mathrm{int}},g^{k}_{\mathrm{int}}]}", from=4-4, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{[g_n^{\bot},g_k^{\bot}]}"', from=4-5, to=3-5]
	\arrow["\jmath"', from=4-5, to=4-4]
	\arrow["{[g^{n}_{\mathrm{ext}},g^{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}]}", from=6-5, to=4-4]
	\arrow["{[\overline{g}^{n}_{\mathrm{ext}},\overline{g}^{k}_{\mathrm{ext}}]}"', from=6-5, to=4-5]
\end{tikzcd}\]
Define
\[
\widehat c_1
\defeq
\left\langle
\top_{\mathcal L^\bot};(c_1)_V
\right\rangle
:
i\longrightarrow\mathcal L^\bot,
\qquad
\widehat c_2
\defeq
\left\langle
\top_{\mathcal L^\bot};(c_2)_V
\right\rangle
:
j\longrightarrow\mathcal L^\bot.
\]
Thus \(\widehat c_1\) and \(\widehat c_2\) have the same vertex
components as \(c_1\) and \(c_2\), respectively, but send the root of
their domain to \(\top_{\mathcal L^\bot}\).
An extended pushout complement is further a \emph{boundary pushout complement} if additionally the following conditions hold:
\begin{enumerate}
  \item match $m$ is mono;
  \item $[c_1, c_2]$ is mono;
  \item if the anchor \(m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})\) is
\(\top_{\mathcal F}\), then
\[
n\coprod j
\xrightarrow{
  \overline g_{\mathrm{ext}}^{\,n}\coprod\id_j
}
(n'\setminus S_n)\coprod j
\xrightarrow{
  [g_n^\bot,\widehat c_2]
}
\mathcal L^\bot
\xleftarrow{
  [g_k^\bot,\widehat c_1]
}
(k'\setminus S_k)\coprod i
\xleftarrow{
  \overline g_{\mathrm{ext}}^{\,k}\coprod\id_i
}
k\coprod i
\]
is a well-typed MDA-extended cospan;

\item if the anchor \(m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})\) is different from
\(\top_{\mathcal F}\), then
\[
n
\xrightarrow{
  \overline g_{\mathrm{ext}}^{\,n}
  \fatsemi\iota_{n'\setminus S_n}
}
(n'\setminus S_n)\coprod j
\xrightarrow{
  [g_n^\bot,\widehat c_2]
}
\mathcal L^\bot
\xleftarrow{
  [g_k^\bot,\widehat c_1]
}
(k'\setminus S_k)\coprod i
\xleftarrow{
  \overline g_{\mathrm{ext}}^{\,k}
  \fatsemi\iota_{k'\setminus S_k}
}
k
\]
is an MDA-extended cospan, not necessarily well typed, where
\(\iota_{n'\setminus S_n}\) and
\(\iota_{k'\setminus S_k}\) are the first coprojections into the
displayed internal interfaces.
\end{enumerate}
\end{definition}
Again, this is a natural generalisation of the usual boundary complement, accounting for the fact that maps $c_1$ and $c_2$ must be re-anchored to correctly map $i$ and $j$ as parts of the internal interfaces.
The extended cospan in condition (4) is not necessarily well-typed because the $\boxsort$-edge containing the image of $i$ and $j$ under $\hat{c}_1$ and $\hat{c}_2$ may not necessarily have enough vertices in its sources and targets to account for these additional interfaces.
What matters is that $j$ and $i$ face input and output interfaces, respectively, so that inputs are eventually glued to outputs and vice versa.

Just like in the case of ordinary boundary pushout complements, the extended boundary pushout complement is unique if it exists.

\begin{proposition}[Uniqueness of extended boundary complements]
\label{prop:extended_boundary_complement_unique}
Let
\[
i\coprod j
\xrightarrow{f}
\mathcal L
\xrightarrow{m}
\mathcal G
\]
be the folded span associated with well-typed MDA-extended cospans as
in the definition of an extended boundary pushout complement. Suppose
that \(m\) is mono.

If
\[
\bigl(
\mathcal L^\bot,c,l^\bot,
g_n^\bot,g_k^\bot,\alpha_n,\alpha_k
\bigr)
\]
and
\[
\bigl(
\mathcal K,d,h,
h_n^\bot,h_k^\bot,\beta_n,\beta_k
\bigr)
\]
are extended boundary pushout complements of the same span, then there
is a unique strict isomorphism
\[
\phi:\mathcal L^\bot\longrightarrow\mathcal K
\]
compatible with all the complement data. Explicitly,
\[
c\fatsemi\phi=d,
\qquad
\phi\fatsemi h=l^\bot,
\]
and
\[
g_n^\bot\fatsemi\phi=h_n^\bot,
\qquad
g_k^\bot\fatsemi\phi=h_k^\bot.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\alpha_n=\beta_n,
\qquad
\alpha_k=\beta_k.
\]
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
Write \(c=[c_1,c_2]\) and \(d=[d_1,d_2]\). The sets
\(S_n\) and \(S_k\) depend only on the rule, the match and the
interfaces of \(\mathcal G\), and are therefore the same for both
complements.

We first compare the carriers. 
By~\cref{lemma:complement_shape}, the image of either
complement in \(\mathcal G\) has edge set
\[
l^{\bot}(E_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}) = \bigl(E_{\mathcal G}\setminus m(E_{\mathcal L})\bigr)
\cup\{m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})\} = h(E_{\mathcal{K}}).
\]

The edge components of \(l^\bot\) and \(h\) are injective. Indeed, the
interface \(i\coprod j\) has only its root edge, so the pushout
identifications cannot identify two distinct edges belonging to the
same complement.

Since $c$ and $d$ are mono, and the concrete pushout construction gives us pushouts on sets of vertices, we can decompose the pushout diagram in terms of images as follows
% https://q.uiver.app/#q=WzAsNixbMywwLCJWX3tpIFxcY29wcm9kIGp9Il0sWzAsMCwiZl9WKFZfe2lcXGNvcHJvZCBqfSkgXFxjb3Byb2QgVl57XFxjaXJjfV97XFxtYXRoY2Fse0x9fSJdLFswLDIsIm0oZihWX3tpIFxcY29wcm9kIGp9KSkgXFxjb3Byb2QgbShWXntcXGNpcmN9X3tcXG1hdGhjYWx7TH19KSBcXGNvcHJvZCBWXntcXGNpcmN9X3tcXG1hdGhjYWx7R319Il0sWzIsMiwiYyhWX3tpIFxcY29wcm9kIGp9KSBcXGNvcHJvZCBWXntcXGNpcmN9X3tcXG1hdGhjYWx7TH1ee1xcYm90fX0iXSxbMywzLCJkKFZfe2kgXFxjb3Byb2Qgan0pIFxcY29wcm9kIFZee1xcY2lyY31fe1xcbWF0aGNhbHtLfX0iXSxbMSwwXSxbMCwzLCJjX1YiLDJdLFswLDQsImRfViJdLFswLDEsImZfViIsMl0sWzEsMiwibV9WIiwyXSxbMywyLCJsXntcXGJvdH1fViIsMl0sWzQsMiwiaF9WIiwyXSxbMiw1LCIiLDIseyJzdHlsZSI6eyJuYW1lIjoiY29ybmVyIn19XV0=
\[\begin{tikzcd}
	{f_V(V_{i\coprod j}) \coprod V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{L}}} & {} && {V_{i \coprod j}} \\
	\\
	{m(f(V_{i \coprod j})) \coprod m(V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{L}}) \coprod V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{G}}} && {c(V_{i \coprod j}) \coprod V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}} \\
	&&& {d(V_{i \coprod j}) \coprod V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{K}}}
	\arrow["{m_V}"', from=1-1, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{f_V}"', from=1-4, to=1-1]
	\arrow["{c_V}"', from=1-4, to=3-3]
	\arrow["{d_V}", from=1-4, to=4-4]
	\arrow["\lrcorner"{anchor=center, pos=0.125, rotate=90}, draw=none, from=3-1, to=1-2]
	\arrow["{l^{\bot}_V}"', from=3-3, to=3-1]
	\arrow["{h_V}"', from=4-4, to=3-1]
\end{tikzcd}~,\]
where we let
\[
V^\circ_{\mathcal L}
\defeq
V_{\mathcal L}\setminus f_V(V_{i\coprod j}),
\qquad
V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}
\defeq
V_{\mathcal L^\bot}\setminus c_V(V_{i\coprod j}),
\qquad
V^\circ_{\mathcal K}
\defeq
V_{\mathcal K}\setminus d_V(V_{i\coprod j}),
\]
and
\[
V^\circ_{\mathcal G}
\defeq
V_{\mathcal G}\setminus m_V(V_{\mathcal L}).
\]
By the explicit pushout construction, no generating identification
involves a vertex of \(V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}\). Hence
\(l^\bot_V\) is injective on \(V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}\), and its
image is precisely \(V^\circ_{\mathcal G}\). Consequently, the
corestriction
\[
l^\bot_V\restriction_{V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}}
:
V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}
\longrightarrow
V^\circ_{\mathcal G}
\]
is a bijection. The same argument applied to the other pushout shows
that the corestriction
\[
h_V\restriction_{V^\circ_{\mathcal K}}
:
V^\circ_{\mathcal K}
\longrightarrow
V^\circ_{\mathcal G}
\]
is a bijection. Therefore
\[
V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}
\cong
V^\circ_{\mathcal K}.
\]

Moreover, since \(c\) and \(d\) are mono,
\[
c_V(V_{i\coprod j})
\cong
V_{i\coprod j}
\cong
d_V(V_{i\coprod j}).
\]
Together with the disjoint decompositions
\[
V_{\mathcal L^\bot}
=
c_V(V_{i\coprod j})\coprod V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot},
\qquad
V_{\mathcal K}
=
d_V(V_{i\coprod j})\coprod V^\circ_{\mathcal K},
\]
this gives
\[
V_{\mathcal L^\bot}\cong V_{\mathcal K}.
\]

For edges, put
\[
W_{E} \defeq l^{\bot}_{E}(E_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}) = h_{E}(E_{\mathcal{K}})~.
\]
Since both maps are injective, their corestriction to $W_{E}$ are bijective, so we let
\[
\phi_{E} \defeq l_{E}^{\bot} \fatsemi (h_{E} \restriction^{W_{E}})^{-1}~.
\]
For vertices, we let
\[
\phi_{V}(x) \defeq
\begin{cases}
d_{V}(z) & x = c_{V}(z),\\
l_{V}^{\bot}(x) \fatsemi (h_{V} \restriction^{V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{K}}})^{-1} & x \in V^{\circ}_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}~.
\end{cases}
\]
These are bijections and satisfy 
\[
c \fatsemi \phi = d, \qquad \phi \fatsemi h = l^{\bot}.
\]
In particular, the second equality on vertices from $V_{i \coprod j}$ reduces to
\[
h_{V}(\phi_{V}(c_{V}(z))) = h_{V}(d_{V}(z)) = m_{V}(f_{V}(z)) = l^{\bot}_{V}(c_{V}(z))~. 
\]

For $e \in E_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}$, we have that
\begin{align*}
h^{*}(s_{\mathcal{K}}(\phi_{E}(e))) &= s_{\mathcal{G}}(h(\phi_{E}(e)))\\
&= s_{\mathcal{G}}(l^{\bot}(e))\\
&= (l^{\bot})^{*}(s_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}(e))\\
&= h_{V}^{*}(\phi_{V}^{*}(s_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}(e)))~.
\end{align*}
Since the original extended cospan is monogamous, the restrictions $f_{V} \restriction_{i}$ and $f_{V} \restriction_{j}$ are injective, therefore $h_{V}$ identifies only vertices $u \in V_{i}$ and $v \in V_{j}$.
Because there must be an MDA-extended cospans with carrier $\mathcal{K}$, $h_{V}$ is injective on sources, since vertices $u$ must be mapped to target vertices of $\mathcal{K}$ and vertices $u$ must be mapped to source vertices.
The same argument applies to targets.

By construction, we have that $\phi_{E}(\top_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}) = \top_{\mathcal{K}}$, since $l^{\bot}$ and $h$ are strict.
It remains to check that $\phi$ preserves $<^{\mu}$.
For every non-root vertex or edge $x$ we have
\begin{align*}
h_{E}(\phi_{E}(<^{\mu}_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}(x))) &= l^{\bot}_{E}(<^{\mu}_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}(x))\\
&= <^{\mu}_{\mathcal{G}}(l^{\bot}(x))\\
&= <^{\mu}_{\mathcal{G}}(h(\phi(x)))\\
&= h_{E}(<^{\mu}_{\mathcal{K}}(\phi(x)))~.
\end{align*}
Injectivity of $h_{E}$ therefore gives
\[
\phi_{E}(<^{\mu}_{\mathcal{L}^{\bot}}(x)) = <^{\mu}_{\mathcal{K}}(\phi(x))~.
\]
Therefore, since $\phi$ is a strict homomorphism and is bijective on vertices and edges, it is an isomorphism.

We next check if 
\[
g_n^{\bot} \fatsemi \phi = h_n^{\bot}, \qquad g_{k}^{\bot} \fatsemi \phi = h_{k}^{\bot}.
\]
Since \(S_n\) and \(S_k\) are the same for both complements, the relevant maps have
types
\[
g_n^\bot\fatsemi\phi,\;h_n^\bot
:
n'\setminus S_n\longrightarrow\mathcal K
\]
and
\[
g_k^\bot\fatsemi\phi,\;h_k^\bot
:
k'\setminus S_k\longrightarrow\mathcal K.
\]
By condition~(4) for the two complements and the equality
\(\phi\fatsemi h=l^\bot\), we have
\[
g_n^\bot\fatsemi\phi\fatsemi h
=
g_n^\bot\fatsemi l^\bot
=
\jmath_n\fatsemi g^n_{\mathrm{int}}
=
h_n^\bot\fatsemi h,
\]
where
\[
\jmath_n:n'\setminus S_n\hookrightarrow n'
\]
is the evident inclusion.

The edge components of \(g_n^\bot\fatsemi\phi\) and \(h_n^\bot\)
are equal because \(h_E\) is injective.

For the vertex components, let \(z\in V_{n'\setminus S_n}\) and put
\[
x\defeq\phi_V(g^\bot_{n,V}(z)),
\qquad
y\defeq h^\bot_{n,V}(z).
\]
We then have
\[
h_V(x)=h_V(y).
\]
If \(x\neq y\), then, there
exist \(u\in V_i\) and \(v\in V_j\) such that
\[
f_V(\iota_i(u))=f_V(\iota_j(v))
\]
and either
\[
x=d_{1,V}(u),\qquad y=d_{2,V}(v),
\]
or vice versa.

The internal input interface maps
\[
[g_n^\bot,\widehat c_2]
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
[h_n^\bot,\widehat d_2]
\]
are monic, since the boundary complements are MDA-extended cospans.
Consequently,
\[
g^\bot_{n,V}(z)\notin\operatorname{im}(c_{2,V}),
\qquad
h^\bot_{n,V}(z)\notin\operatorname{im}(d_{2,V}).
\]
Therefore, under the assumption \(x\neq y\), the only remaining
possibility is
\[
x=d_{2,V}(v),\qquad y=d_{1,V}(u).
\]
But then
\[
\phi_V(c_{2,V}(v))
=
d_{2,V}(v)
=
x
=
\phi_V(g^\bot_{n,V}(z)).
\]
Injectivity of \(\phi_V\) gives
\[
c_{2,V}(v)=g^\bot_{n,V}(z),
\]
contradicting
\(g^\bot_{n,V}(z)\notin\operatorname{im}(c_{2,V})\).
Hence \(x=y\). Since \(z\) was arbitrary, and the edge components
are also equal, we conclude that
\[
g_n^\bot\fatsemi\phi=h_n^\bot.
\]

The output case is dual, using monicity of
\[
[g_k^\bot,\widehat c_1]
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
[h_k^\bot,\widehat d_1],
\]
and gives
\[
g_k^\bot\fatsemi\phi=h_k^\bot.
\]

Finally, consider the comparison isomorphisms of condition~(5).
The re-anchored maps
\[
\widehat g_n^{\,a}:S_n\longrightarrow\mathcal G,
\qquad
\widehat g_k^{\,a}:S_k\longrightarrow\mathcal G
\]
depend only on the original cospan, the match and its anchor, and are
therefore the same for both complements. Condition~(5) gives
\[
\alpha_n\fatsemi\widehat g_n^{\,a}
=
f_{\mathrm{int}}\restriction_{i'_\circ}\fatsemi m
=
\beta_n\fatsemi\widehat g_n^{\,a}.
\]
On vertices, this equality reads
\[
(\alpha_n)_V
\fatsemi
g^n_{\mathrm{int},V}\restriction_{V_{S_n}}
=
(\beta_n)_V
\fatsemi
g^n_{\mathrm{int},V}\restriction_{V_{S_n}}.
\]
Since \(g^n_{\mathrm{int},V}\) is injective, we obtain
\[
(\alpha_n)_V=(\beta_n)_V.
\]
Moreover, both \(\alpha_n\) and \(\beta_n\) are isomorphisms of pointed
discrete e-hypergraphs and therefore agree on the unique root edge.
Consequently,
\[
\alpha_n=\beta_n.
\]
The same argument applied to
\[
\alpha_k\fatsemi\widehat g_k^{\,a}
=
f_{\mathrm{int}}\restriction_{j'_\circ}\fatsemi m
=
\beta_k\fatsemi\widehat g_k^{\,a}
\]
gives
\[
\alpha_k=\beta_k.
\]

For uniqueness, let
\(\psi:\mathcal L^\bot\to\mathcal K\) be another isomorphism satisfying
\[
c\fatsemi\psi=d,
\qquad
\psi\fatsemi h=l^\bot.
\]
The first equality determines \(\psi_V\) on
\(c_V(V_{i\coprod j})\). Since \(\psi_V\) is bijective, it maps
\(V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}\) into \(V^\circ_{\mathcal K}\), where
\(h_V\) is injective. The second equality therefore determines
\(\psi_V\) on \(V^\circ_{\mathcal L^\bot}\). It also determines
\(\psi_E\), since \(h_E\) is injective. Consequently,
\(\psi=\phi\), as their action agrees on all vertices and edges.
\end{proof}

\subsection{Convex decomposition and rewriting}
If $\mathcal{L}$ is a convex sub-e-hypergraph of $\mathcal{F}$, we can formulate the analogue of~\cref{lemma:convex_decomposition}.
\begin{lemma}[Recursive convex decomposition]
\label{lemma:extended_convex_decomposition}
Let
\[
\mathbf F
=
\left(
n
\xrightarrow{f_{\mathrm{ext}}}
n'
\xrightarrow{f_{\mathrm{int}}}
\mathcal F
\xleftarrow{g_{\mathrm{int}}}
k'
\xleftarrow{g_{\mathrm{ext}}}
k
\right)
\]
be a well-typed MDA-extended cospan, and let
\[
m:\mathcal L\longrightarrow\mathcal F
\]
be an anchored mono whose image is convex in the underlying hypergraph
of \(\mathcal F\). Assume moreover that the image of \(m\) is closed
under successors below every matched non-root edge: for every
\(e\in E_{\mathcal L}\setminus\{\top_{\mathcal L}\}\) and every
\(x\in V_{\mathcal F}\coprod E_{\mathcal F}\), 
\[
m_E(e)<^\mu_{\mathcal F}x
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x\in\operatorname{im}(m),
\]
that is, condition (2) of~\cref{def:extended_complement} holds.

Then the occurrence of \(\mathcal L\) in \(\mathbf F\) admits a finite
recursive decomposition by composition, tensor and join.
More precisely, the following hold.

\begin{enumerate}
\item Suppose that
\[
m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})=\top_{\mathcal F}.
\]
Then there are \(i,j,q\in\mathbb N\), a well-typed MDA-extended
cospan
\[
\mathbf L
=
\left(
i
\xrightarrow{l_{\mathrm{ext}}}
i'
\xrightarrow{l_{\mathrm{int}}}
\mathcal L
\xleftarrow{r_{\mathrm{int}}}
j'
\xleftarrow{r_{\mathrm{ext}}}
j
\right),
\]
and well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\[
\mathbf C_1
=
\left(
n
\xrightarrow{c^1_{\mathrm{ext}}}
n_1'
\xrightarrow{c^1_{\mathrm{int}}}
\mathcal C_1
\xleftarrow{d^1_{\mathrm{int}}}
k_1'
\xleftarrow{d^1_{\mathrm{ext}}}
q\coprod i
\right)
\]
and
\[
\mathbf C_2
=
\left(
q\coprod j
\xrightarrow{c^2_{\mathrm{ext}}}
n_2'
\xrightarrow{c^2_{\mathrm{int}}}
\mathcal C_2
\xleftarrow{d^2_{\mathrm{int}}}
k_2'
\xleftarrow{d^2_{\mathrm{ext}}}
k
\right)
\]
such that there exists an isomorphism $\mathbf{\Phi}$:
\[
  \mathbf{\Phi}:
  \mathbf C_1
  \fatsemi
  \bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathbf L\bigr)
  \fatsemi
  \mathbf C_2
  \overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}
  \mathbf F.
\]
where
\[
\mathbf 1_q
=
\left(
q\xrightarrow{\id_q}q\xrightarrow{\id_q}q
\xleftarrow{\id_q}q\xleftarrow{\id_q}q
\right)
\]
is the identity extended cospan on \(q\).
Let $\iota_{\mathcal{L}} : \mathcal{L} \xrightarrow{} \mathcal{C}_{1} \fatsemi (q \otimes \mathcal{L}) \fatsemi C_2$ be the canonical map induced by the coprojections into the pushouts computing the composite.
Then
\begin{equation}
\iota_{\mathcal{L}} \fatsemi \phi = m,
\tag{$\ast\ast$}
\label{eq:convex_decomposition_isomorphism_factors}
\end{equation}
where $\phi$ is the carrier component of the isomorphism $\mathbf{Phi}$.

\item Suppose that
\[
a\defeq m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})
\neq\top_{\mathcal F}.
\]
Let \(p\) be the unique \(\boxsort\)-edge satisfying
\[
p<^\mu_{\mathcal F}a.
\]
Then the anchor \(a\) determines a component cospan
\[
\mathbf F_a:u\longrightarrow v
\]
of \(p\), and \(m\) corestricts to a strict mono
\[
m_a:\mathcal L\longrightarrow\mathcal F_a.
\]
The image of \(m_a\) is convex and satisfies the same successor-closure
condition. Hence the first case gives the decomposition
\[
  \mathbf{Phi}_a:
  \mathbf C_1^a
  \fatsemi
  \bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathbf L\bigr)
  \fatsemi
  \mathbf C_2^a
  \overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}
  \mathbf F_a
  \tag{\(\dagger\)}
  \label{eq:extended-convex-component-iso}
\]
for suitable well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\[
\mathbf C^a_1:u\longrightarrow q\coprod i,
\qquad
\mathbf C^a_2:q\coprod j\longrightarrow v,
\]
such that
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^a
  \fatsemi
  \phi_a
  =
  m_a,
  \tag{\(\dagger\dagger\)}
  \label{eq:extended-convex-component-occurrence}
\]
where \(\iota_{\mathcal L}^a\) is the canonical occurrence of
\(\mathcal L\) in the source of \(\phi_a\).

Writing
\[
\mathbf P
=
\mathbf F_a
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\\
d\neq a
}}
\mathbf F_d,
\]
the occurrence of \(\mathcal L\) is therefore exposed inside the
\(a\)-component as
\[
\mathbf{D}_p = \bigl(
\left(
\mathbf C^a_1
\fatsemi
(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathbf L)
\fatsemi
\mathbf C^a_2
\right)
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F}(p)\\
d\neq a
}}
\mathbf F_d\bigr).
\]
There is an induced isomorphism
\[
  \mathbf{Phi}_p:
  \mathbf D_p
  \xrightarrow{\cong}
  \mathbf P
  \tag{\(\ddagger\)}
  \label{eq:extended-convex-box-iso}
\]
whose component at the distinguished \(a\)-th operand is
\(\phi_a\) and whose other components are identities. If
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^p:
  \mathcal L
  \longrightarrow
  \mathcal{D}_p
\]
is the resulting canonical occurrence, then
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^p
  \fatsemi
  \phi_p
  =
  m_p,
  \tag{\(\ddagger\ddagger\)}
  \label{eq:extended-convex-box-occurrence}
\]
where \(m_p:\mathcal L \to \mathcal{P}\) is the
corestriction of \(m\).

If \(p\) is itself nested, the sub-e-hypergraph generated by \(p\) and
its components is convex in its enclosing component, and the same
construction is applied recursively to that occurrence. Each
application moves one level towards \(\top_{\mathcal F}\), so the
process terminates.
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Suppose first that the anchor of \(m\) is
\(\top_{\mathcal F}\). Apply
\cref{lemma:convex_decomposition} to the underlying MDA-cospan and the
convex image of \(\mathcal L\). It supplies hypergraphs
\(\mathcal C_1,\mathcal C_2\), a number \(q\) of through wires and a
factorisation of the underlying cospan as
\[
\mathcal C_1
\fatsemi
\bigl(\id_q\otimes\mathcal L\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathcal C_2.
\]

The input and output interfaces \(i\) and \(j\) of \(\mathcal L\)
are given by the boundary vertices in $\mathcal{F}$, and internal interfaces $i'$ and $j'$ are given by $i$ and $j$ adjoined with the blocks $\pi^{-1}_{f}(e)$ and $\pi_{g}^{-1}(e)$ for each $\cmpsort$-edge $e$ in $m(\mathcal{L})$.
The remaining interface
blocks of \(\mathcal F\) restrict in the same way to
\(\mathcal C_1\) and \(\mathcal C_2\), giving the interfaces
\(n_1',k_1',n_2',k_2'\) displayed above.

The successor-closure assumption ensures that no matched
\(\boxsort\)-edge is separated from any of its components. Consequently,
complete internal interface blocks are assigned to
\(\mathbf C_1\), \(\mathbf L\) or \(\mathbf C_2\); no block is split
between them. The induced extended cospans are therefore well typed.
They are monogamous and acyclic by the same argument as in
\cref{lemma:convex_decomposition}. 
Finally, the composite is canonically isomorphic to \(\mathbf F\):
\[
  \mathbf{Phi}:
  \mathbf C_1
  \fatsemi
  (\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathbf L)
  \fatsemi
  \mathbf C_2
  \overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}
  \mathbf F.
\]
By construction of the convex decomposition, \(\phi\)
carries the canonical occurrence
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}:
  \mathcal L
  \longrightarrow
  \operatorname{car}
  \left(
    \mathcal{C_1}
    \fatsemi
    (q \otimes \mathcal{L})
    \fatsemi
    \mathcal{C_2}
  \right)
\]
to the given match \(m\). Hence
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}
  \fatsemi
  \phi
  =
  m.
\]

Now suppose that
\[
  a=m_E(\top_{\mathcal L})\neq\top_{\mathcal F}.
\]
As above, \(m\) corestricts to a strict match
\[
  m_a:\mathcal L\longrightarrow\mathcal F_a
\]
which is convex and successor-closed. Applying the root case inside
\(\mathbf F_a\) gives the isomorphism
\[
  \mathbf{\Phi}_a:
  \mathbf C_1^a
  \fatsemi
  (\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathbf L)
  \fatsemi
  \mathbf C_2^a
  \overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}
  \mathbf F_a
\]
satisfying
\[
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^a
  \fatsemi
  \phi_a
  =
  m_a.
\]

Reinsert this decomposition into the distinguished \(a\)-component
of \(p\), leaving every other component unchanged.
Join preserves isomorphisms, so we have
\[
  \mathbf{\Phi}_p:\mathbf D_p \xrightarrow{\cong} \mathbf P.
\]
Let
\[
  \jmath_a:\mathcal F_a \longrightarrow \mathcal P
\]
be the canonical inclusion of the \(a\)-component. 
By the componentwise
construction of \(\mathbf{Phi}_p\),
\[
\begin{aligned}
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^p
  \fatsemi
  \phi_p
  &=
  \iota_{\mathcal L}^a
  \fatsemi
  \phi_a
  \fatsemi
  \jmath_a\\
  &=
  m_a\fatsemi\jmath_a\\
  &=
  m_p.
\end{aligned}
\]

The subcospan \(\mathbf P\) generated by \(p\) and its components is
convex and successor-closed in its enclosing component. We may
therefore apply the root case to its occurrence there, and then, if
necessary, reinsert the resulting decomposition into the next
enclosing box. At every stage the new isomorphism is obtained from the
preceding one by composition, tensor product or join with identity
isomorphisms, so the same calculation shows that it carries the
canonical occurrence of \(\mathcal L\) to the corresponding
corestriction of \(m\).

Each iteration moves one level towards \(\top_{\mathcal F}\). Since
the hierarchy is finite, the process terminates and yields the required
recursive decomposition, together with an isomorphism whose carrier
component carries the canonical occurrence of \(\mathcal L\) to the
original match \(m\).
\end{proof}
The above lemma plays the same role as~\cref{lemma:outermost_box_decomposition} in the proof of completeness of rewriting.

\begin{definition}[Convex EDPOI rewriting]
\label{def:convex_edpoi_rewriting}
The EDPOI rewriting as presented in~\cref{def:dpoi_rooted} is \emph{convex} if the the image of the match $m$ is a convex sub-e-hypergraph of $\mathcal{G}$ and the extended pushout complement is a boundary pushout complement.
Given a rule $\langle \mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R} \rangle$, we write
\[
\mathcal{G} \widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{\langle \mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R} \rangle} \mathcal{H}
\]
and say that $\mathcal{G}$ \emph{convexly} rewrities to $\mathcal{H}$.
\end{definition}

\subsection{The presentation and rewriting theorems}
Let
\[
\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)/\mathfrak R
\]
denote the quotient by the restriction of the structural congruence
\(\sim_{\mathfrak R}\) to well-typed MDA-extended cospans. 
Since \(\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)\) is closed under composition, tensor and
join, this quotient inherits the structure of an
\(\catname{SLat}\)-PROP.

\begin{theorem}
\label{theorem:mehypi_slat_isomorphism}
There is an isomorphism of \(\catname{SLat}\)-PROPs
\[
\mathbf S_{\Sigma}^{\join}
\cong
\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)/\mathfrak R.
\]
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
The embedding \(\Xi\) restricts to an identity-on-objects strict
symmetric monoidal functor
\[
\widetilde\Xi:
\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma)
\longrightarrow
\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma).
\]
Indeed, if \(F\) is monogamous and acyclic, then the box-free
extended cospan \(\widetilde\Xi(F)\) is also monogamous and acyclic.

The proof of~\cref{lemma:canonical_representative} restricts to
\(\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)\). The only additional point to check is
that the components of the resulting canonical representative belong
to \(\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma)\). 
This follows from the restriction we have on internal interface maps: restricting to a component restricts the internal interface to the potion of the interface belonging to that component, and since the component has no $\boxsort$-edges, it corresponds to an ordinary MDA-cospan.
Consequently, every morphism of the
quotient has a unique canonical representative of the form
\[
\widetilde\Xi(F_1)\join\cdots\join\widetilde\Xi(F_p),
\qquad
F_1,\ldots,F_p\in\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma),
\]
with no two \(F_i\) isomorphic.

It follows that the mutually inverse \(\catname{SLat}\)-functors
constructed in the proof of
\(\cref{theorem:ehypi_slat_isomorphism}\) restrict to mutually inverse
isomorphisms
\[
\widetilde F_{\catname{SLat}}
\bigl(\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma)\bigr)
\cong
\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)/\mathfrak R.
\]
Finally, the isomorphism of PROPs
\[
\llangle-\rrangle:
\mathbf S_{\Sigma}
\longrightarrow
\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma)
\]
from~\cref{theorem:monogamous_isomorphism} induces, by free
\(\catname{SLat}\)-enrichment, an isomorphism
\[
\mathbf S_{\Sigma}^{\join}
\cong
\widetilde F_{\catname{SLat}}
\bigl(\catname{MHypI}(\Sigma)\bigr).
\]
Composing these two isomorphisms gives the required result.
\end{proof}

As in the Frobenius case, convex rewriting must be considered modulo
the structural rules. For well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\(\mathcal A,\mathcal B:m\to n\) and
\(\mathcal L,\mathcal R:i\to j\), write
\[
\mathcal A
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}^{\,\mathfrak R}_{
  \langle\mathcal L,\mathcal R\rangle
}
\mathcal B
\]
if there exist well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\(\mathcal A',\mathcal B',\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\) such that
\[
\mathcal A\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal A',
\qquad
\mathcal B'\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal B,
\qquad
\mathcal L\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal L',
\qquad
\mathcal R'\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal R,
\]
and
\[
\mathcal A'
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal B'
\]
is a convex EDPOI rewrite.

Notice that this relation is defined on the original MDA-extended
cospans. The combined external-interface maps
\[
i\coprod j\longrightarrow\mathcal L',
\qquad
i\coprod j\longrightarrow\mathcal R'
\]
are used only in the EDPOI diagram of \cref{def:dpoi_rooted}. They are
not regarded as extended cospans in their own right. In particular, we
do not require the folding of an MDA-extended cospan to be MDA, nor do
we repackage such a folding and subsequently attempt to recover the
original extended cospan.

Write
\[
I_{\mathrm M}:
\mathbf S_\Sigma^{\join}
\longrightarrow
\catname{MEHypI}(\Sigma)/\mathfrak R
\]
for the isomorphism of
\cref{theorem:mehypi_slat_isomorphism}. The function
\(\mathrm{Rep}\) on raw terms restricts to a function taking raw
\(\mathbf S_\Sigma^{\join}\)-terms to well-typed MDA-extended cospans,
and satisfies
\[
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(t))=I_{\mathrm M}([t]).
\]

\begin{theorem}[Soundness and completeness of convex EDPOI rewriting]
\label{theorem:convex_edpoi_rewriting_slat}
Let \(a,b:m\to n\) and \(l,r:i\to j\) be raw
\(\Sigma^{\join}\)-terms for \(\mathbf S_\Sigma^{\join}\). Then
\[
[a]\Rightarrow_{\langle[l],[r]\rangle}[b]
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\mathrm{Rep}(a)
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}^{\,\mathfrak R}_{
  \left\langle
    \mathrm{Rep}(l),
    \mathrm{Rep}(r)
  \right\rangle
}
\mathrm{Rep}(b).
\]
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}
We first prove the forward implication by induction on the derivation of
\[
[a]\Rightarrow_{\langle[l],[r]\rangle}[b]
\]
in \cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}.

Suppose first that the rewrite is witnessed by the first decomposition
in \cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}. Choose raw representatives
\(a_1\) and \(a_2\) for the two morphisms appearing in that
decomposition. For some \(k\), we then have
\[
[a]
=
\left[
a_1
\fatsemi
(\id_k\otimes l)
\fatsemi
a_2
\right]
\]
and
\[
[b]
=
\left[
a_1
\fatsemi
(\id_k\otimes r)
\fatsemi
a_2
\right].
\]
Applying \cref{lemma:rewriting_congruence} to the rewrite $\mathrm{Rep}(l) \widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{\langle \mathrm{Rep}(l), \mathrm{Rep}(r) \rangle} \mathrm{Rep}(r)$ 
gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\mathrm{Rep}(a_1)
\fatsemi
\bigl(\id_k\otimes\mathrm{Rep}(l)\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathrm{Rep}(a_2)
\\
&\qquad
\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathrm{Rep}(l),\mathrm{Rep}(r)\rangle
}
\mathrm{Rep}(a_1)
\fatsemi
\bigl(\id_k\otimes\mathrm{Rep}(r)\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathrm{Rep}(a_2).
\end{aligned}
\]
Here the rewrite supplied by
\cref{lemma:rewriting_congruence} is convex. 
By compositionality of \(\mathrm{Rep}\), the source and target of this
rewrite are
\[
\mathrm{Rep}\bigl(
a_1\fatsemi(\id_k\otimes l)\fatsemi a_2
\bigr)
\]
and
\[
\mathrm{Rep}\bigl(
a_1\fatsemi(\id_k\otimes r)\fatsemi a_2
\bigr),
\]
respectively. Compatibility with denotation gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(a))
&=
I_{\mathrm M}([a])
\\
&=
I_{\mathrm M}
\left(
\left[
a_1\fatsemi(\id_k\otimes l)\fatsemi a_2
\right]
\right)
\\
&=
Q_{\mathrm M}
\left(
\mathrm{Rep}\bigl(
a_1\fatsemi(\id_k\otimes l)\fatsemi a_2
\bigr)
\right),
\end{aligned}
\]
and similarly for \(b\). Hence the endpoints of the direct rewrite
are structurally equivalent to \(\mathrm{Rep}(a)\) and
\(\mathrm{Rep}(b)\), as required.

Suppose now that the rewrite is witnessed by the second decomposition
in \cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}, and consider the smaller rewrite
\[
[a']\Rightarrow_{\langle[l],[r]\rangle}[b'].
\]
By the induction hypothesis,
\[
\mathrm{Rep}(a')
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}^{\,\mathfrak R}_{
  \langle\mathrm{Rep}(l),\mathrm{Rep}(r)\rangle
}
\mathrm{Rep}(b').
\]
Choose witnesses
\[
\mathcal F',\mathcal G',\mathcal L',\mathcal R'
\]
for this rewrite. Thus
\[
\mathcal F'
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal G'
\]
is a direct convex EDPOI rewrite, with the four displayed cospans
structurally equivalent to the corresponding interpretations.

Let \(\gamma\) be a raw representative of the join of the unchanged
components, and choose raw representatives for the two outer
morphisms displayed in the second decomposition. Apply the join case
of \cref{lemma:rewriting_congruence} to replace
\(\mathcal F'\) by \(\mathcal G'\) while leaving the components
represented by \(\gamma\) unchanged. Then apply the tensor and
composition cases of the same lemma to reinstate the displayed outer
morphisms. The resulting rewrite is convex.
Since
\(\sim_{\mathfrak R}\) is a congruence, the resulting endpoints are
structurally equivalent to \(\mathrm{Rep}(a)\) and
\(\mathrm{Rep}(b)\). This proves the forward implication.

Conversely, choose well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\[
\mathcal F',\mathcal G',\mathcal L',\mathcal R'
\]
witnessing
\[
\mathrm{Rep}(a)
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}^{\,\mathfrak R}_{
  \langle\mathrm{Rep}(l),\mathrm{Rep}(r)\rangle
}
\mathrm{Rep}(b).
\]
Thus
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathrm{Rep}(a)&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal F',
&
\mathcal G'&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathrm{Rep}(b),\\
\mathrm{Rep}(l)&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathcal L',
&
\mathcal R'&\sim_{\mathfrak R}\mathrm{Rep}(r),
\end{aligned}
\]
and there is a direct convex EDPOI rewrite
\[
\mathcal F'
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal G'.
\]
Let
\[
m:\mathcal L'\longrightarrow\mathcal F'
\]
be its match. Since the rewrite is convex, \(m\) is an anchored mono
with convex image. It also satisfies the successor-closure condition
because its pushout complement is an extended pushout complement.
Thus all the hypotheses of
\cref{lemma:extended_convex_decomposition} hold.

Suppose first that the match anchor is the root. The decomposition
lemma gives well-typed MDA-extended cospans
\(\mathcal C_1,\mathcal C_2\) and some \(q\) such that
\[
\mathcal F'
\cong
\mathcal C_1
\fatsemi
\bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathcal L'\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathcal C_2.
\]
Define
\[
\mathcal G''
\defeq
\mathcal C_1
\fatsemi
\bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathcal R'\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathcal C_2.
\]

Suppose instead that the match is nested. Using the notation of the
second part of \cref{lemma:extended_convex_decomposition}, we first
have
\[
\mathcal F_a
\cong
\mathcal C^a_1
\fatsemi
\bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathcal L'\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathcal C^a_2.
\]
Put
\[
\mathcal G_a
\defeq
\mathcal C^a_1
\fatsemi
\bigl(\mathbf 1_q\otimes\mathcal R'\bigr)
\fatsemi
\mathcal C^a_2.
\]
Replacing \(\mathcal F_a\) by \(\mathcal G_a\) in
\[
\mathcal F_a
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F'}(p)\\
d\neq a
}}
\mathcal F_d
\]
gives
\[
\mathcal G_a
\join
\bigjoin_{\substack{
d\in\operatorname{Comp}_{\mathcal F'}(p)\\
d\neq a
}}
\mathcal F_d.
\]
If \(p\) is itself nested, apply the next decomposition supplied by
\cref{lemma:extended_convex_decomposition} and make the same
replacement in the corresponding enclosing component. Repeating this
at every enclosing \(\boxsort\)-edge eventually reaches the root.
Denote the resulting well-typed MDA-extended cospan by
\(\mathcal G''\).

Starting with the identity application
\[
\mathcal L'
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal R'
\]
and applying \cref{lemma:rewriting_congruence} to each of the concrete
compositions, tensors and joins appearing in the above decomposition
gives a direct convex EDPOI rewrite
\[
\mathcal F'
\;\widetilde{\rightsquigarrow}_{
  \langle\mathcal L',\mathcal R'\rangle
}
\mathcal G''.
\]

By~\cref{lemma:convex_decomposition} the original match $m$ factors through the isomorphisms induced by the decomposition and since the extended boundary complement is unique we have
\[
\mathcal{G}'' \cong \mathcal{G}'~.
\]
% Because the decomposition was constructed from the original
% occurrence \(m\), this rewrite uses the same match \(m\) as the given
% rewrite. Its extended boundary pushout complement is obtained by
% removing precisely that occurrence from the concrete decompositions
% above. It is therefore an extended boundary pushout complement for
% the same folded span and with the same retained interfaces as the
% complement in the given rewrite.

% By \cref{prop:extended_boundary_complement_unique}, the two
% complements are uniquely isomorphic. Pushing out
% \(\mathcal R'\) along these isomorphic complements produces
% isomorphic resulting extended cospans. Hence
% \[
% \mathcal G''\cong\mathcal G'.
% \]

Now put
\[
\begin{aligned}
a'&\defeq
I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\bigl(Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal F')\bigr),
&
b'&\defeq
I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\bigl(Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal G'')\bigr),\\
l'&\defeq
I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\bigl(Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal L')\bigr),
&
r'&\defeq
I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\bigl(Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal R')\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\]
Applying \(I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\) to each concrete decomposition above
gives the corresponding decomposition in
\(\mathbf S_\Sigma^{\join}\). The decomposition at the root gives the
first clause of \cref{def:enriched_prop_rewriting}. In the nested
case, replacing \(\mathcal F_a\) by \(\mathcal G_a\) gives the second
clause, and every subsequent enclosing \(\boxsort\)-edge gives another
application of that clause. It follows that
\[
a'\Rightarrow_{\langle l',r'\rangle}b'.
\]

Finally,
\[
\begin{aligned}
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal F')
&=Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(a))
 =I_{\mathrm M}([a]),\\
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal L')
&=Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(l))
 =I_{\mathrm M}([l]),\\
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal R')
&=Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(r))
 =I_{\mathrm M}([r]),
\end{aligned}
\]
and
\[
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal G'')
=
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathcal G')
=
Q_{\mathrm M}(\mathrm{Rep}(b))
=
I_{\mathrm M}([b]).
\]
Applying \(I_{\mathrm M}^{-1}\) gives
\[
a'=[a],
\qquad
b'=[b],
\qquad
l'=[l],
\qquad
r'=[r].
\]
Therefore
\[
[a]\Rightarrow_{\langle[l],[r]\rangle}[b],
\]
as required.
\end{proof}

We thus have shown the equivalence of convex EDPOI rewriting and term rewriting in $\mathbf{S}^{\join}_{\Sigma}$.
This concludes our combinatorial characterisation of string diagrams for free symmetric monoidal $\catname{SLat}$-categories, instead of being a different notation for term syntax, string diagrams are now represented as concrete e-hypergraphs with interfaces which enables mechanisation of said rewriting above.

\begin{remark}
The above development generalises to enriched coloured PROPs, where objects are lists of types, rather than just natural numbers.
This way, each vertex will be assigned a label.
\end{remark}

\section{Applications}

At the beginning of this chapter we saw how a fragment of e-graphs can be
represented using string diagrams, and hence using e-hypergraphs. This
representation serves as a justification of adequacy for our proposal: since the
framework captures a fragment of e-graphs, it is reasonable to use it as a
foundation of equality saturation for monoidal theories. As we also saw, once
sharing is taken into account, enriched string diagrams have different semantics
from traditional e-graphs, so the framework can equally be regarded as a
foundation for \emph{substructural} e-graphs.

The crucial property of the hypergraph structures in the context of monoidal
theories is that they absorb many structural equalities which in traditional
e-graphs must be applied explicitly---this is what made them inefficient in this
context in the first place. We now illustrate the principle on the ZX-calculus,
which is a good test case precisely because a large part of its equational theory
is structural.

\subsection*{The ZX-calculus}

The ZX-calculus is a graphical language introduced by Coecke and
Duncan~\cite{coecke_interacting_2011} for reasoning about quantum computation, with diagrams
denoting linear maps between Hilbert spaces---not necessarily unitary, so
quantum circuits form a proper subclass. Read as a monoidal theory, it is
presented by two Frobenius algebras~\cref{def:frobenius_prop} which interact with one
another as a bialgebra~\cref{fig:bialgebra_generators_equations}. Each algebra contributes one generator,
the green and red \emph{spiders}.

We restrict attention to the phase-free fragment and work up to nonzero scalars.
This is not a bare truncation: phase-free ZX is exactly the calculus of
$\mathbb{F}_2$ linear relations, with green spiders imposing equality on their
legs and red spiders even parity, and it is complete for that fragment. It is
also the fragment in which every rule below is stated without side conditions on
phases. We adopt the horizontal orientation traditional for ZX-diagrams rather
than the vertical one used elsewhere in this thesis.
We use the following encoding of the spiders.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/z_spider} \qquad \tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/x_spider}
}
\]
The encoding takes place in a coloured PROP, in the sense of~\cref{def:coloured-prop}: objects are lists of colours drawn from $
\left\{
\adjustbox{scale=1}{
\begin{tikzpicture}
    \begin{pgfonlayer}{nodelayer}
        \node [style=Z dot] (0) at (0, 0) {};
    \end{pgfonlayer}
\end{tikzpicture}
},
\adjustbox{scale=1}{
\begin{tikzpicture}
    \begin{pgfonlayer}{nodelayer}
        \node [style=X dot] (0) at (0, 0) {};
    \end{pgfonlayer}
\end{tikzpicture}
}
\right\}
$.
This encoding is what allows us to absorb the structural equalities of the Frobenius algebra on each sort: a vertex of one colour \emph{is} a spider of that colour, of whatever arity its incidences dictate.
This is not the encoding usually adopted for the ZX-calculus, which is the open graphs of Dixon and Kissinger~\cite{dixonOpengraphsMonoidalTheories2013, ZX}, where spiders of both colours are ordinary vertices; we adopt the coloured one here because absorbing the structural equalities on both colours at once is precisely the phenomenon we wish to illustrate.

What this buys is best seen on the famous rule allowing two spiders of the same colour to be fused, diagrammatically represented as
\[
\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/z_fusing_example}~.
\]
The encoding absorbs this rule, in the sense that all spiders in our diagrams are already fused: two same-coloured spiders joined by a wire are not two vertices but one, so the rule never has to be applied.
The Frobenius structure of each colour is thus paid for once, in the representation, rather than repeatedly, in the rewrite system.

The price is that the two colours can no longer be composed directly: a green wire and a red wire are different objects, so nothing in the syntax joins a green spider to a red one.
To do that, we need to introduce colour-changing hyperedges, which are the only hyperedges our diagrams contain.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
  \tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/z-to-x-edge} \qquad \tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/x-to-z-edge}
}
\]
Strictly, the wires should be coloured too, since it is the colours of the objects rather than of the vertices that prevent direct composition.
We leave them uncoloured so as not to overload the pictures: the colour of every wire is determined by the vertex it is incident to, so no information is lost.

What remains to be rewritten is exactly what the representation cannot absorb.
Consider the following rules
\begin{equation}
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_id_rule}
}
\label{eq:zx_id_rule}
\tag{1}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_bialgebra_rule}
}
\label{eq:zx_bialgebra_rule}
\tag{2}
\end{equation}
\begin{equation}
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_hopf_rule}
}~.
\label{eq:zx_hopf_rule}
\tag{3}
\end{equation}
These correspond to the ZX identity, bialgebra and Hopf rules respectively.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
  \tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_rules_string}~.
}
\]
It is worth noting how differently the three behave. Rule~\eqref{eq:zx_id_rule} deletes a vertex
and merges its neighbours. 
Rule~\eqref{eq:zx_bialgebra_rule} deletes both of its vertices, and is
consequently indexed by their arities: an instance is determined by how many
neighbours each of the two vertices has. 
Rule~\eqref{eq:zx_hopf_rule} deletes only edges, so both
its vertices survive and it applies at any arity whatsoever.
\subsection*{A worked example}
Now consider the diagram below.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{
  \tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_diagram_example}~.
}
\]
Two features of it matter. 
First, there is no redex for the Hopf rule~\eqref{eq:zx_hopf_rule}
anywhere: no two vertices are joined by more than one edge. Whatever Hopf
redexes eventually appear will have to be manufactured. Second, the diagram has
exactly one internal green vertex---every other green vertex is named by the
boundary interface. Since rules~\eqref{eq:zx_id_rule} and~\eqref{eq:zx_bialgebra_rule} both delete a vertex, and DPOI
rewriting requires the interface to factor through the pushout complement, every
rewrite available anywhere in this diagram consumes that one vertex. A
destructive rewriter therefore gets a single choice, and the choices are not
equivalent: one of them reaches the normal form and the others terminate early.

This is what the $\catname{SLat}$-structure is for, once we consider the corresponding freely $\catname{SLat}$-enriched PROP. 
Idempotence lets us replace a
subdiagram $a$ by the join $a \join a$, held in a $\boxsort$-edge, and then rewrite
one of the two copies while the other is retained unchanged. 
Alternatively, we can consider non-destructive rules, that is, rules of the form $\langle l, l \join r \rangle$, but we choose to explicitly apply idempotence in what follows.


Finding the redex for rule~\eqref{eq:zx_id_rule}, applying idempotence and then applying the
identity rule itself yields the diagram below. Note how the $\boxsort$-edge
represents both alternatives without duplicating the whole diagram: everything
outside the rewritten region is shared between the two branches. From here on we
display the embedding of the internal interfaces only where it would otherwise
be ambiguous, to reduce clutter.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_diagram_example_step_1}}~.
\]
Applying structural rewrites again, we can reveal the redex for the bialgebra
rule~\eqref{eq:zx_bialgebra_rule}. 
This is the step the example is built around: the redex is on the
branch that rule~\eqref{eq:zx_id_rule} would have destroyed, and it is still there because the
earlier rewrite was non-destructive.
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_diagram_example_step_2}}~.
\]
Applying the rule gives the diagram: 
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_diagram_example_step_3_2}}~.
\]
Observe that the result is larger than what it replaced: the bialgebra rule trades two vertices for a complete bipartite graph between their neighbours, so a strategy guided by diagram size would have had no reason to choose it.
What it buys is immediate---two of the edges it introduces run between vertices that were already adjacent, which makes two redexes for the Hopf rule~\eqref{eq:zx_hopf_rule} visible at once, and we apply them next:
\[
\adjustbox{max width=\textwidth}{\tikzfig{./figures/monoidal_egraphs/zx_diagram_example_step_4}}~.
\]
This does not saturate the whole diagram: there are still redexes for bialgebra rules, and of course, the identity rule can be applied right to left.
This is no different from traditional e-graphs, some set of rewrite rules may not guarantee the termination of equality saturation, so one has to apply heuristics to determine when to stop.
Altought, in the case of e-graphs, in some cases it is possible to guarantee that equality saturation terminates~\cite{tree_automata_e_graphs}.
\subsection*{Discussion}
Occasionally large portions of the diagram are duplicated, since some rules
touch most of it; but even then sharing is preserved to some extent---the untouched regions are never copied and once a rewrite rule is applied, common substructures can be factored out. 
Matching modulo
$\catname{SLat}$-structure also appears practically feasible: it is not necessary to
unfold portions of the diagram, as a traversal may follow the interfaces into
$\boxsort$- and $\cmpsort$-edges and recurse inside, with the interfaces
stipulating where the traversal should continue.

We make no claim that this is a competitive approach to quantum circuit
optimisation. That is a substantial research area with mature tooling and
heuristics of its own, and a serious comparison would demand far more than a
worked example. The purpose here is narrower: to show that the structural
equations of a real monoidal theory can be absorbed into the representation, potentially gaining benefit from this absorption and non-destructive nature of rewriting once some clever algorithmics is in place. 
Hopefully, this example also suggests that the alternative of using traditional e-graphs is not viable: even encoding spiders within e-graphs term language would be a difficult task.
\section{Related work}

Rooted e-hypergraphs belong to the established family of hierarchical
(hyper)graph formalisms. 
Existing approaches capture parts of the
structure required here to various extents, including nested subgraphs, parent assignments,
interfaces, and categorical support for rewriting. 
None, however, provides unchanged the particular combination needed to represent joins in a
semilattice-enriched symmetric monoidal category. 
Moreover, our design was highly influenced by hypergraphs of Bonchi et al.~\cite{bonchi_string_2022-1}, because their work supplied the necessary ingredients for us to state the necessary correspondences.
A bespoke specialisation was therefore necessary. We briefly
review the closest existing formalisms and explain how they differ from the
structure used here.

Drewes, Hoffmann and Plump define a hierarchical graph recursively as a
top-level graph in which distinguished frame edges each contain one further
hierarchical graph~\cite[Defs.~2.1--2.2]{plump:hierarchical-graphs}.
Our $\cmpsort$- and $\boxsort$-edges are the direct analogues of their frames.
Their hierarchical morphisms correspond to our strict homomorphisms: they
act level by level, mapping the top-level graph to the top-level graph and
recursively mapping the contents of a frame to the contents of its image.
Applications below the top level are handled by recursively invoking the
rewriting relation inside a frame, rather than by admitting anchored or lax
morphisms into the whole host graph.
This is too restrictive for our internal interfaces, where vertices may be mapped to arbitrarily nested vertices in the target e-hypergraph, and for our notion of a DPOI rewriting.

Palacz's formalism is closest to ours at the level of carriers.  Starting
from an arbitrary category of flat graphs, it equips the set of vertices and
edges with an acyclic parent-assignment function and a formal root
\(\top\)~\cite[Defs.~1 and~4]{palacz:hierarchical-transform}.  Its morphisms
preserve immediate parents: root-level morphisms send \(\top\) to \(\top\),
whereas nesting morphisms send it to an atom of the target and thereby place
the whole source beneath a common parent.  These correspond, respectively, to
our strict and anchored homomorphisms.  The formalism is nevertheless too
general at the level of objects and too restrictive at the level of maps to be
used unchanged.  
Both edges and vertices are allowed by be predecessors under the parent function and moreover edges may connect vertices at different levels of the hierarchy.
Conversely, every Palacz morphism preserves each immediate-parent step, so
there is no counterpart of our lax, ancestor-preserving maps, which are needed
for internal interface legs.  

Castelnovo, Gadducci and Miculan take a different, explicitly categorical
route.
Their hierarchical hypergraphs with interface consist of a tree order on the
set of hyperedges, an otherwise independent incidence map, and a map from an
interface set to the vertices; the resulting category is
adhesive~\cite[Def.~3.5 and Thm.~3.4]{castelnovoNewCriterion}.
This gives a powerful generic setting for DPO rewriting, but only hyperedges
participate in the hierarchy.
It therefore does not record which component owns a vertex, nor does its tree
order distinguish boxes from alternative components.

Alvarez-Picallo, Ghica, Sprunger and Zanasi use hierarchical hypergraphs to
give a combinatorial representation of string diagrams for monoidal closed
categories~\cite{fscd}.  
Their parent maps assign vertices and edges either to
an enclosing edge or to an implicit outermost level, and they require every
edge and its incident vertices to have the same parent~\cite[Def.~16]{fscd}.  
Their restricted objects, called hypernets, are
monogamous and acyclic hypergraphs in which an unlabelled edge contains the
single subdiagram represented by a monoidal-closed abstraction; 
these hypernets are moreover equipped with cospan interfaces~\cite[Defs.~17--19]{fscd}.
Their internal and external interfaces are represented as subsets of a single cospan interface.
This representation identifies the relevant interfaces of an individual hypernet, but two hypernets with with the same external intrerface need not be composable as ordinary cospans.
This is the place where our construction of extended cospans comes into play: we explicitly distinguish between internal and external interfaces, so that cospans are composable along external interfaces.
We also specify how internal interfaces are carried through composition.

The same refinement is useful for DPOI rewriting.
Alvarez-Picallo et al.\ require only the outermost interfaces of the two sides
of a rule to agree and describe the resulting behaviour of internal interfaces
\cite[Def.~21 and Prop.~22]{fscd}.
Our EDPOI construction makes this bookkeeping explicit at the level of
cospans: it removes from the host interface the internal interfaces belonging
to the matched left-hand side and adjoins the internal interfaces belonging to
the right-hand side.
Furthermore, their hierarchy has no distinguished root edge, so a non-strict
morphism need not place the images of elements with undefined predecessor
under a common parent.
We obtain this coherence by requiring a match to be anchored.
Notably, they require the image of the match to be \emph{down-closed}; we
adopt this requirement in the form of condition~(2)
in~\cref{def:extended_complement}.
Our construction can therefore be viewed as a refinement of their approach, supplying categorical account of hierarchical interfaces and performing necessary bookkeeping during composition and EDPOI rewriting; we furthermore make sure that all the required pushouts exist in our setting.

\section{Conclusion}
